ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u2	Dear President, colleagues and fellow Members, good afternoon! Position of the Group of the Parties of the Modern Centre to the Proposal for a Recommendation of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia concerning coherent regional development and the creation of conditions for new rural jobs, EPA 1856-VII. Ambassador Andrej Chuš is 21. 3. 2017 submitted a proposal for a recommendation to the Government of the Republic of Slovenia in the legislative procedure. Day 10. 5. In 2017, we received a proposal to withdraw the proposal from the National Assembly. The withdrawal proposal indicated that the Member has an indeterminate obligation at the local level. At the regular meeting of the Committee on the Economy, we have nevertheless considered the recommendation. The Ministry of Economy convinced us with its argument. We have not supported or followed the opinion of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia and will do so today. The MEP believes that in more than 30 years we have failed to take a sufficient step towards reducing regional differences. It believes that state centralism needs to be replaced by decentralisation and transfer of responsibility for development to local level. The provincial level must assume responsibility for the preparation and management of development programmes and the state must set up appropriate controls. The proposer also considers that the great potential for preserving and creating new jobs in Slovenia is mainly rural, especially in the area of agriculture, and that the Government does not implement the Harmonised Regional Development Act, which consequently leads to rural extinction and urban escape. The response of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia sent to the National Assembly shows that this 29 September 2016 adopted the Strategy for the Development of Local Self-Government in the Republic of Slovenia by 2020. The main objective of the strategy is to strengthen local self-government and consequently improve the quality of life at local level. Regional policy is mainly implemented in Slovenia in the area of the Law on the Promotion of coherent regional development. Efforts to reduce development differences between regions are linked to reducing development differences and the eastern and western cohesion regions during this programming period. Unlike the previous period, greater emphasis is placed on promoting entrepreneurship and maximising the benefits of existing infrastructure projects. How many development projects will be co-financed under the Eastern Cohesion Region funds will depend primarily on two factors – the quality of projects that will run in public tenders and the possibility of coordination by the region of the proposed projects. In particular, support will be given to those types of activities that will create conditions for the creation of jobs or an additional source of rural income in connection with the addition of wood value. It will be necessary to establish as long a forest chain as possible – we have been talking about this for a long time and things are moving more slowly – local self-sustainment, green tourism, natural and cultural heritage and traditional knowledge, social entrepreneurship, social security services, management of organic waste and renewable energy sources. Thank you.

Ladies and gentlemen, there are 400 000 children in the Republic of Slovenia, of which about 15% are children who have risk factors in development. When we discussed this law in a broad discussion at the round tables we organised, experts and I found that this percentage is still significantly higher, that there were about 25% of such children because medicine had progressed, because parents were also more and more older, because there are many factors and these children are much more visible; so, these children have parents, they have relatives. Somehow, if we find that these children who have risk factors in development are somewhere between 40 and 60 000 and then multiply this number by the number of family members, we get some tenth of the population of the Republic of Slovenia, which is concerned by this law, because this child is not alone, I have my own family, which is its primary support unit. I think it's very important that we deal with this issue. If you read newspapers, if you look at social networks, you see many times the parents who literally some of them scream in a cry for help because they often feel alone in this position when they have such a child. Many times this screaming is justified, perhaps sometimes not, but the common thing is that it is often true that the actual child, the family, is taught several times on its own because it does not have the right support, the right help. That is why I also tried to help myself 10 years ago or more, as far as I know and know from my profession as a lawyer, and I wrote a Guide to the Rights of Children with Special Needs. That's why this law is about helping these families and these kids. The purpose of the law is to ensure the protection of the child, to treat the child multidisciplinaryly, as well as the entire family. Furthermore, the purpose of the law is to link the existing services we have. Of course, it is not that we have nothing, we have individual early treatment services, but these early treatment services and some other services need to be linked into a single system. We have certain health services, and then we have rights in the field of social protection and education and education. This matter needs to be linked to the whole and provided to the family with support and information. The aim of the law is also to extend existing capacities to provide early treatment services to children and families. Therefore, to extend the development clinics in which these children are treated, and also to provide funding by the State for the development departments of kindergartens. Ladies and gentlemen, the law before us is, of course, a compromise, because that is always the case when we decide on a law that is in Parliament. We could not follow all our desires, even though the original text was somehow such a collection of desires. We can only take on preschool education because of the various factors that I will then talk about. We couldn't get the early-treatment institute I wanted, and so on. What is essential is that we have pursued the aim of ensuring the protection of the general group of children and also that all children will be sifted through this system and that a systemic solution is provided for children. In my opinion, the law is necessary, it is necessary for children, and I therefore thank all those who have contributed signatures to the law and will continue to support it. Thank you very much.

I must say, however, that we did not sit at the crossroads of time in this area, and we have already proposed or held a meeting in 2015 and 2016 with the assistance of the Health Committee on the issue of integrated treatment of children. I believe that this has also helped to speed things up in this area. As far as we're concerned. In Slovenia, early treatment of children with developmental disorders is regulated only by the Health and Health Insurance Act, which does not specify what this treatment covers, how it is organised and how it is accessible. In practice, this aid is mostly carried out by healthcare homes. In Slovenia we do not yet have clear system arrangements for early treatment of children with special needs, but we do have different forms of early treatment, which are not conceptually and functionally related. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, we agree that early treatment is the most important in the development of a child with special needs, but also of course for others, and it must then be addressed with particular care and integrity. Early treatment of children can prevent many problems further, i.e. during the school period and in later periods of life. The proposed law identifies the shortcomings of early treatment of children with special needs in the preschool age. As a severe obstacle, it lists waiting types for developmental outpatients and proposes to transform them into development centres. Thus, between the objectives, for example, the conversion of development ambulances to early treatment centres within a network that will be publicly accessible and regionally distributed; the provision of integrated treatment for children with special needs, for children with risk factors and their families from birth to entry into school – for example, early treatment services, the creation of multi-disciplinary teams and the identification of their tasks -; the integration of early treatment centres with professionals in kindergartens, social work centres and so on. I don't think there's eleven goals listed so I won't list all of them. All we can say in general is that they are not in line with the existing Child Guidance Act with special needs, as this law already regulates the existence of child guidance centres with special needs. In addition, the current law deals with these children from the point of view of the functioning of everyday life in the family and the main part of the worker in educational institutions, kindergartens or schools. The law basically treats a person as an entity, an active and learning creature and from this function is looking for the best therapeutic solutions for a child. The proposed law, however, treats the child from the point of view of a doctor, a therapist, or treatment facility. In our view, it is pointless to have two laws and two completely different approaches for one person. We advocate early treatment, which helps a person in his active functionality, in active life and learning, where therapy is only part of this treatment and assistance, and the second part is learning how to live and learn. We believe that some good solutions would make sense to integrate into the existing law on children with special needs. For example, the proposed law introduces new centres, which is a duplication for us, because the centres already exist under the ZUPP. As an example, let me say, for example, the Center Cirius Kamnik in Vipava, which includes treatment and education. It would also be appropriate to include preschool children. Teams in these institutions include both educators, teachers, pediatricians, doctors, specialists. What is really necessary is the extension of their exposures, of course, which would allow reasonable local accessibility for all children, but in particular for preschools. Thus, the tasks of such centres could be enlargement, such as ensuring the integrated treatment of children with special needs for children with risk factors and their families from birth to completion of schooling and career guidance, identifying treatment services from early treatment to guidance in kindergarten, school and vocational guidance, linking a pediatrician and guidance for early monitoring and assistance to the family and so on. In the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, we believe that existing resources and capacities and knowledge must be exploited, as proper allocation would aim to help children and adults with special needs while at the same time not raising the costs of services. We will support the law in the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group, although we believe it would be more reasonable to correct and improve the existing Child Guidance Act with special needs.

ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u14	Dr. Murgle, I'm sorry, I'm a little angry and upset about what happened. For we are the ones who are enacting laws in this country, but in the ministries there are those who have to implement them and I assure you that they will have to do so. In Slovenia, care for children with special institutional needs is organised in the form of a network of health institutions and child care institutions. Those who have never had personal experience therefore imagine that adequate care should be provided within this network to ensure that children receive sufficient early, quality, regular and frequent treatment and that their families receive adequate support. The reality is quite different. A family with one or more children with special needs often remains alone, without real information about mental and material distress, isolated from society, with the stigma prescribed to their child by society. Parents themselves carry the terrible burden of anxiety, self - perceptions, translate them into a spectrum of emotions, and seek answers to questions that often do not have answers. As parents often encounter misunderstandings in health and social welfare institutions, they seek support from NGOs, which in the field of child care with a number of special needs, but also these NGOs are not all-powerful, all-knowing and often too limited by financial means, so that they can always effectively jump to help. The fact is that every child with special needs has its own needs, its own challenges and its own pace of development, so it often happens that children with similar diagnostics cannot be helped in the same way as parents and caregivers are still under pressure. All of us who have ever met in the field of dealing with children with special needs, one through personal experience, another through professional experience or just because of empathy, have repeatedly warned that we urgently need a well-organised, multidisciplinary, family-oriented early treatment of this group of children. While developing outpatients, specialised outpatients and developmental wards are trying to cover the early-treatment programme, they are limited to occasional checks and development controls, which is usually not sufficient. Someone described working with children with special needs as a marathon — progressing slowly, with small steps, no rest, and therefore more occasional examinations are usually not helpful. In the Desus Group, we believe that it is necessary to adopt a specific law that will clearly and specifically define the early treatment of children with special needs, ensure the right to early and quality multidisciplinary treatment, identify beneficiaries, scope and services in the community and commit competent authorities to develop a comprehensive and sustainable early treatment system. For these reasons, we have also contributed signatures under the law. By adopting the law, we believe that we will provide modern, integrated and sustainable forms of early treatment for children with special needs and for their families, at least partially eliminating the backlog compared to other EU Member States, where such a programme has been known and implemented for many years. Early support for children of this group is urgently needed because early childhood intensive brain development, which lasts up to the age of six, ensures the acceleration of the development of the child with developmental backlogs and reduces the possibility of developing their permanent special needs. Appropriate early treatment also brings benefits for later life, as it reduces the need for special education, the likelihood of institutionalisation and ensures the development of the capacity for as independent and independent a life in society as possible. What surprised us at the drafting of the bill was the opinion of the Ministry of Health, which points out that the term “child with special needs” does not exist at all in the treatment of disease situations. There may not be an expression, but children exist, no matter what we call them. This seems unacceptable to us from the Ministry, which should play one of the leading roles in the early treatment of children. Moreover, the Ministry of Health even believes that we do not need any specific legislation for children with special needs. When a person reads such a position, he understands why an autistic child has to wait more than two years for a dentist; not just an autistic one, this applies to all children with special needs. You can probably imagine the damage, in a particular case only on teeth, caused by such a long waiting period — in most cases irreparable. In our parliamentary group, unlike the Ministry of Health, we believe that a special law is needed, namely the one before us. With it, we will allow for an integrated treatment in the multidisciplinary teams, children and families will receive the necessary support, even if the family wants it, which is called a family representative in the bill, and will have a personal experience in the field of early child treatment.

ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u23	Dear Mr President, the distinguished Madam Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues, welcome! We have before us the Law on Early Treatment of Children with Special Needs, for which we are already looking forward to the end of the legislative process and the start of application. All those who have any connection with children with special needs and who have been professionally involved with them or through NGOs are particularly aware of the urgent need for this law. This area is not systematically regulated as early support for newborns and young children is scattered across different institutions. Parents travel from specialist to specialist, at longer intervals, meaning that they do not receive timely and, above all, appropriate systematic treatments, nor do they receive instructions to deal with such assistance to the children in need. At the same time, an expert and continuous comprehensive treatment of the child will enable the child to develop his or her abilities better and prevent problems from escalating. Developed countries have had this area regulated for decades, and it is high time for us to join it, as a study of early treatment of children with special needs in EU Member States has shown that Slovenia is the only member who does not have this programme prescribed by law. Article 26 of the Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities, Rehabilitation and Rehabilitation, which obliges our country to ensure that, as far as possible, people with disabilities live independent and healthy lives, that rehabilitation is ensured at the earliest possible stage and is based on a multidisciplinary assessment of the needs and ability of each individual to be as close to the home of the user and his family as possible, that staff are qualified to provide quality services and that adequate facilities are available. The right to early treatment and multi-disciplinary permanent treatment is, in principle, enshrined in Article 12 of the Law on the Equalization of the Possibilities of Persons with Disabilities, but this legal norm has not yet been fulfilled for us. We therefore need a specific law that will clearly and concretely define early treatment, ensure the right to this treatment, identify beneficiaries, the extent and services of the community and oblige decision makers to develop a comprehensive and permanent early treatment system. For more than 20 years, NGOs have been working for a well-organised multi-disciplinary and family-oriented early treatment of this group of children, but failing because it always stops at money. But this law is absolutely necessary. In Slovenia, there are about 400 000 children between the ages of zero and 19 years of age and almost 10 to 15 children have special needs or different forms of developmental disturbance. So, finally, the law is before us. We will, of course, support it in our parliamentary group, because it is imperative that we address this grey fox properly and take a step forward together. The purpose of the law is to connect existing assistance in Slovenia into a single system and to complement it with the most substantive activities within the treatment of the smallest children, not focusing only on the child, but also on the entire family in which the child lives. It is very important that the pool of experts in the development clinic is expanded and multi-disciplinary approaches addressed. Of course, early marriage will not solve all the problems and challenges faced by people with special needs and their parents, but it certainly gives more than what we have now. In the initial phase of the pilot project, it is envisaged that a network of nine early-treatment centres will be provided and that, in the further phase, better accessibility and regional distribution will require treatment in all 21 outpatients. The establishment of early treatment centres will enable parents to get better acquainted with and empower their child’s entire treatment and support as soon as possible. Psychosocial assistance is of paramount importance to the family, which the law foresees, since parents are often left to themselves and especially lack the attention and assistance of experts. Family representatives selected from NGOs will also be a great help to families. According to our parliamentary group, the law is very broad and well established, but it needs the support of all three ministries; both health, education and social. Each of them must take up his share in behalf of those to whom the law is addressed. The Early Treatment Act is intended for the integrated treatment of children during the period when it is possible to achieve the most in its development. What is lost during this development period can have difficult remedial consequences in later life. We must be aware of the need to invest in prevention, as underdevelopment is not necessarily a permanent situation and early identification, treatment and timely action are essential to prevent further underdevelopment. Promoting child development in the earliest years means much more than that. It means that certain children will have far fewer problems in later development periods, to be able to develop skills and functional skills that will help them to a greater degree of autonomy and to enter the social and educational process more easily. For all of this, we will, of course, support in our Group the conclusion that the law is suitable for further consideration. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u25	Thank you for your word, chairman. In the United Left, we support this bill. We agree with the proposaler that the quality and comprehensive early treatment of development problems is of paramount importance for the further development of the child. In the early stages of the child, it is possible to achieve the greatest progress and developmental breakthroughs, so quality assistance to children and parents is essential for later optimal development. The quality and comprehensive early treatment of children with special needs also reduces the later costs associated with special regular education, institutionalisation and social distress of families. It is crucial that the problems identified in early childhood development do not contribute to the discrimination and exclusion of children from regular education. Here, too, we agree with the view that socio-economic factors condition the various challenges of children's disability and lead to inequalities in health. By improving services and making early recognition, treatment and integrated assistance available to children with developmental disorders and their parents, equality and social justice are ensured to the greatest extent possible. The existing arrangements for the development of outpatients have proved to be insufficient in practice. Due to its professional limitations, the scope of the aid has also been limited, and professional work has sometimes proved inadequate, which has also led to dissatisfaction on the part of parents. We therefore support the upgrading of the current system of development ambulances into a publicly accessible and regionally distributed network of early treatment centres. As an important aspect of the upgrade, we focus on upgrading existing working groups with psychologists, educators and social workers. The current early treatment focuses in particular on the health care of the child and does not include social protection and assistance to parents. Well, this current proposal provides for the creation of multidisciplinary teams capable of comprehensive diagnostics of child development disorders on the one hand and assistance to the family on the other. The proposal enjoys broad support both in the National Assembly and in civil society. It has also been co-designed and supported by disability societies, which believe that early treatment centres, as envisaged by the proposal, will provide regular systematic and multidisciplinary treatment for children and their social environment. Since the proposal is also consistent with the competent ministries, we see no reason why the law would not live well in practice. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u26	Mrs Iva Dimic will present the position of the New Slovenia Group - Christian Democrats.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u27	Dear President, Honourable Secretary of State, colleague, colleague, proposer! In Slovenia, after the current arrangements for early treatment of children with special needs, they are held in the development clinics, and in practice it has proved repeatedly that health workers in the development clinics are not qualified enough to help the parents of these children and to provide professional and emotional support. Early treatment should focus primarily on the family, the mother, the father and also the child, as it aims to provide optimal support for the development of children and to improve the quality of life of the family. Early treatment is the right of every family and child, and funding must be adequately addressed. The child must be adopted in his family, and the family also needs psychosocial assistance and professional support in the home environment. In Novi Slovenije we have written in our programme and, of course, we also place the family as the center and foundation of society. The family has an irreplaceable role in the upbringing of children, so all Members of the New Slovenia Group have also signed the proposal for this law. The aim of the bill is to ensure an integrated treatment of children with special needs in early treatment entry points, which is at early treatment centres. To this end, the law provides for the reinforcement of the number of health personnel and the extension of staff in these clinics by professionals who are not part of the health care system, that is, a special teacher and, where appropriate, other experts. The extended development clinics will become early-treatment centres, where children will also be treated by an expert in education and education and social workers, along with health professionals. It is proposed to deal individually with the needs of the child and the whole family by drawing up an individual assistance plan for the whole family, not just the child. Individual treatments will be carried out at an early treatment centre and others outside it, in other institutions, in particular in early treatment groups and in other institutions or institutions that are already providing early treatment services. The coordinated implementation of the Family Assistance Plan will be taken care of by the coordinator, who will connect the Early Treatment Centre, kindergarten, the Centre for Social Work and other institutions dealing with children or families. The coordinator will also inform the family of the various forms of assistance available to him, in particular in the area of social protection. In the New Slovenia Group, we really hope that in these 21 envisaged outpatients the treatment will be quick and effective and parents will not feel alone and alone, that they must look for and open doors to all institutions, but really to get service in one place, help in one place. In caring for children with special needs, it is essential that their problems or special needs be identified as soon as possible and that appropriate assistance be provided to children and parents as soon as possible, so that we will unanimously support the proposal of the New Slovenia Group. In Novi Slovenije we are pleased that, following the unanimously adopted Personal Assistance Act submitted by Members of New Slovenia, we are now considering the law on integrated and early treatment of preschool children with special needs and we want to be supported by all members of the National Assembly. We are also prepared to participate actively in the preparation of the Long-Term Care Act. However, the package of adoption of all three laws will allow for an integrated treatment, social security and human dignity for anyone who already needs or will need it.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u28	Mr Mirjam Bon Klanjšček will present the position of the Group of Unaffiliated Members. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u29	Thank you very much. Dear fellows and fellows, a very nice greeting to all! Let today's debate on the law on the comprehensive early treatment of preschool children with special needs begin with a song by Drobtinica Toneta Pavčka. It goes like this: "You're in the world to look at the sun. You're in the world to follow the sun. You are in the world, that the sun itself, and that you cast away the shadow from the world." If you ask your children’s parents with special needs, there is little to say that they have a special sun at home; the sun that shined after their parents overcame the shadows, the shadows, doubt, worries all the way to disappointment. And that is the essence of the law that we are considering today; that we as a society help our parents to deal with all the emotions and efforts they face in raising and caring children with special needs. For even more than children, proper family guidance is needed to be in harmony with their child’s differences and to be able to support him in his life and development. As everything in society changes, it is fortunate that the attitude toward the different people who have special needs also changes. In ancient Greece and up to the 18th century, children and adults, if they had survived childhood at all, were ridiculed, pushed to the social side; in short, they were left to themselves. Since the 18th century, institutions have been established where people with mental disabilities have received medical treatment, but they have been institutionalized mainly for the sake of public peace as a means of assistance. Organised care for children with special needs in Slovenia has grown intensely since World War II. Initially, the curriculum for schools was adapted, and only in the second half of the 20th century did developmental kindergartens occur. And it looks like we're back at the turning point. There is an increasing number of speeches among experts about the treatment of not only a child but also a family with special needs. In practice, it appears that the family is the first and most important factor that stimulates or inhibits the development of children with their views on life and activity. Around the world, children with special needs, together with their families, are already receiving multi-disciplinary early treatment, and we also want to follow these trends in the Slovenian space and this initiative, and we also support them in the Group of Non-Related Members. The Law on Integrated Early Treatment of Pre-school Children with Special Needs changes its approach to children with special needs and their families. From the multi-disciplinary teams just mentioned, which will be available to the family and its specificities from the start, to the integration of NGOs with a rich practice in helping families with children with special needs. To this end, the law allows every family under consideration to have two advisers, the family representative and the family coordinator. The representative of the family will be from non-governmental organisations. The condition stated by the law is that it is someone who is also the parent of a child with special needs or risk factors. Its task will be to provide emotional, moral, and psychological support to the family and to mediate between the family and the profession. In addition, he provides for the law of the co-ordinator of the family, who will be a member of the multi-disciplinary team. Lawmakers propose that it be from an area where the family will be most in need of assistance. The coordinator will take care of the implementation of an individual plan designed by a multidisciplinary team. Given that the tasks of the representative and the coordinator will be intertwined and will interact several times in the same areas where the family will need support, we hope in our Group that their work and attitude towards professional decisions will be unified and coordinated, even though the family representative is meant as an independent person. At least what we would like is for the family to face new dilemmas, where the representative and coordinator of the various opinions on how to deal with the child and the family will take place. We understand that the bill's proponents wanted to provide families with the strongest and most numerous support, but the fact that, by the amount of involvement of different people, including a pediatrician, a educator and an expert group in kindergarten, there is also a growing diversity of views and opinions on the same subjects. Help to the family must be coordinated, different professional opinions should not be resolved within the family, but rather before contacting them. The next dilemma we have in our parliamentary group is the composition of the Government's Coordination Council. Due to the difficulty of dealing with children or families with children with special needs, it is mentioned in the law that three ministries will participate: the Ministry responsible for social affairs, health and education. It is absolutely essential that they participate in the consideration of experts from their field and that this cooperation of ministries is necessary. However, we have concerns that the Government's Coordination Council is composed only of ministers. So there are no representatives of non-governmental organisations, i.e. practitioners, in the roof body, which will monitor the implementation of early treatment and where important decisions will be taken to further develop work in this area. The law has given NGOs an important role and has put the Government's partner's position in the early treatment of children with special needs, and we believe that such institutions should also be members of the Coordination Council. Regardless of these dilemmas, we will support the law in the Group of Non-Aligned Members, because we believe that it is for such human and charitable work as working with children with special needs and their families, working for people who strive for ever better practice and will be motivated by the law to join forces to help children and families. Finally, I would like to expand my view of today's subject. The present law focuses on the treatment of preschool children, but as every child grows up, children with special needs grow up. Among them are those who, despite their differences, want to do some work. Some are able to do more than routine work, and because of their shortcomings, they have lost their jobs or are sentenced only to monotonous labor. In the Group of Non-Aligned Members, we would also like to support a government proposal that will also provide for adults with special needs to be included in society and to stay as an equal part of it. Thank you very much.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u30	Mr Saša Tabakovic will present the position of the Group of the Party of the Modern Centre. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u31	Mr President, thank you for your floor. Dear colleagues and colleagues, representatives of the government! Today, at the initiative of 61 members of the esteemed National Assembly, we are considering the proposal for a law on the integrated early treatment of preschool children with special needs, which is a very important milestone for ensuring adequate treatment of people with special needs in society. The comprehensive early treatment of children — not just preschools, but all — with special needs is not properly regulated today. Unfortunately, the current picture is such that persons with special needs do not receive satisfactory medical care or care to meet their needs. They are also more difficult to integrate into the education system, which also puts them in a deprived position with regard to employment opportunities or the possibility of receiving higher incomes, while high financial expenditure on personal support, health services and various medical devices remains. The main substantive solutions provided for in the bill relate to the provision of early-treatment services and the conversion of developmental outpatients to early-treatment centres where multi-disciplinary teams will work: a medical pediatrician, a graduate nurse, a mid-term nurse, a neurophysiother, a working therapist, a clinical therapist, a clinical psychologist, a specialist pedagogist and a social worker. Among other things, the proposal also provides for the identification of early-treatment service providers and their connection through the assistance coordinator to the family, the way in which they are identified and sent to the early-treatment centre, the provision of assistance to parents with family representatives, the identification of possible content of the individual assistance plan to the family, the integration of the early-treatment centre with kindergarten professionals and social work centres, and the abolition of guidance procedures for pre-school children. This means that the proposal for a law following the recommendations of the World Health Organization for children with special needs and for children with risk factors and their families during the preschool period provides and upgrades health, social protection and education services. Persons with special needs, with additional emphasis on pre-school children, will have new access to all systems and services that will enable them to develop adequately, to have full access to health, education and education services and to social protection services. Since integrated treatment reduces the likelihood of later institutionalisation of these people, as it also provides the basis for developing their capacities for as independent a life in society as possible, we will support the SMC proposal for a law. Thank you very much.

The Centre for Early Treatment, as I said, connects with both kindergarten and the competent centre for social work. That's all I could say under the line. The law, of course, pursues the objective of providing and upgrading health, social care and education services for children with special needs, for children with risk factors and, of course, their families during the pre-school period. The main solutions to the law are to transform the development ambulance into an early treatment centre. It is a publicly accessible and regionally distributed network. This will also ensure a comprehensive legal treatment for children with special needs, for children with risk factors and their families from birth to entry into school. The law will identify early-treatment service providers and then their links through the assistance coordinator to the family, which will provide information to parents and performers for each child. In this way, it will also identify the very early treatment of children and how to identify and send them to an early treatment centre. Within these centres, a multi-disciplinary team will be formed, where their task will be further defined. The possible content of an individual family assistance plan will also be identified, simply because the needs for each child are of course different. I also think it's good that these centers will then connect with the professionals to kindergartens and social work centres. We are also very much talking in this mandate about long waiting lines and time limits for medical treatment. Here I would like to point out that, at present, about 18,000 children are being treated in the development clinics each year, about 900 in the infirmary and, of course, due to the specific development of the brain, it is important that the developmental neurological treatment begins as soon as possible, at least until 3 months of age. In the development clinics, as a consequence, they seek to have children in line for a review within at least one month, which, of course, limits only the number and shorter time of checks, which is therefore not good, since then any further necessary therapy may be delayed or not frequent enough. Therefore, in this context, I also welcome the law because the law proposes a solution to transform, as it is said, the development ambulance into a centre for early treatment of children, which then provides single entry points for integrated treatment and is thus also ensured to shorten time gaps in the treatments required, especially since these entry points are multidisciplinary. And that, as it were, is just one of the solutions. Of course, such a child needs a whole range of additional incentives so that from the start he can somehow compensate for his or her, say, birth deficit, and, on the other hand, parents will somehow try to ease their daily routine. Here I would like to stress further that it has not been forgotten that there are also remote places where children also need this treatment and have, in some ways, been neglected because of their geographical position. This will also make it possible for smaller regional units, which will somehow have well-trained therapists, to try to address this, if I may say so, regional or geographical question. The bill is certainly not a cure for all, of course we are aware of that probably all who are present today at this hearing. In Slovenia there are around 40,000 to 60,000 children with risk factors in development; 7% are infants, followed by children with postpartum complications, infections and so on; around 8 000 to 12 000 are children with mental impairment; children with cerebral paralysis are between 800 and 600; about 4,000 are children with autism; and between 100 and 200 children have epilepsy. Then there are children with hearing impairment and visual impairment, children with congenital disorders of the motion apparatus, with developmental impairment of coordination, and with impaired speech development. Their numbers are expected to increase. All these groups of children are treated in development clinics. In addition to the above mentioned groups of children, children with neuromuscular, neurometabolic and other neurological disorders and children after head injury are also considered in the developmental clinics. The fact that we do not know the exact number of children with special needs or children with risk factors in development tells us much about how this area is being cared for today. Although we have a network of healthcare institutions and institutions in Slovenia that are supposed to take care of this activity, they do not respond to the problems of these children and their parents. The needs of children and their parents are also not adequately met by the development ambulances, mental and hygiene departments or other specialised clinics and kindergarten development departments in Slovenia, which carry out some early childhood treatment programmes. The lack of existing early assistance to families with children with special needs is also the fact that the aid is provided by different laws or is not legally provided. There is also no transparent coordination of the necessary assistance. The existing network of development ambulances should therefore be linked with services and institutions operating in the fields of social protection and education and education. For the integrated treatment of children with special needs, the clinical pathways and treatment of children with developmental disorders and the protocols for the cooperation of health, education and social protection services should be further defined. The Law on the Integrative Early Treatment of Preschool Children is therefore intended to solve the problems of these children. Following the introduction of the Act on the integrated early treatment of pre-school children with special needs, there would therefore no longer be the current child guidance process, but at the Centre for the Early Treatment of Children with Special Needs or Risk Factors, a plan would be drawn up for an individual child to support the child and the family in different areas of life. The initial absence of a government representative and all three competent ministries, which are still working hard to bring the law to life in practice, is an unusual journey to the proposal of the law, but it points out that cooperation between ministries will still need to be strengthened. As early childhood is most important for human development, vulnerable children and children with special needs should be treated as soon as possible. That is why, in the Desus Group, we favour the law and we will vote that the bill is suitable for further consideration. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u75	Thank you so much for saying that! Dear colleagues and colleagues, Mr Secretary of State, who has just joined us! The proposal for a law on amendments and additions to the Authority Act and the Bank Resolution Fund, which is before us today, is mainly due to amendments to the Bank Resolution and Forced Cessation Act. The Authority Act and the Bank Resolution Fund make it necessary to comply with the Bank Resolution and Forced Cessation Act. The most important changes relate to the harmonisation of the provisions governing the purpose of the use of the funds of the bank resolution fund. The assets of the bank resolution fund will be able to be used for, for the first time, the payment of the capital of the company to which the funds, rights and liabilities of the bank are transferred in the process of forcible liquidation. Secondly, the payment of compensation to the bank where the fund as the acquirer assumes the assets, rights and obligations of the bank in the course of the compulsory liquidation process. Thirdly, the granting of loans of guarantees, guarantees or other forms of insurance relating to measures of compulsory liquidation. When we discuss the bank resolution fund, the main issues concern the Fund's ability to prevent bank holes and taxpayers' assets from being patched up. Of course, the need for bank resolution also depends on the irregularities that occur in credit institutions and in the banking system. In Slovenia, we have unfortunately had very negative experience in the area of irregularities in the banking system, as we have been abandoned by all the related mechanisms. The effectiveness of the prosecution authorities and law enforcement authorities in dealing with bank crimes is also said to be insufficient. It seems that only the parliamentary investigative committee is seriously involved in the investigation of irregularities in banks in Slovenia. On 22 November last year, the National Assembly voted on the conclusion of the interim report on the parliamentary inquiry, which contained, inter alia, the text, and I quote: “The National Assembly, in line with the essential findings of the parliamentary inquiry, proposes to the Government that it provide legal and materially for the establishment of a specialised court for the prosecution of banking crime within the framework of an organised court.” End of story. 74 members voted in favour of the motion, and no one voted against it. In Novi Slovenija we took the issue of abuse in the banking system very seriously and, on the basis of the previously cited decision, proposed a amendment to the Law on Courts, which, according to a unanimous decision, established specialised judicial departments to deal with banking crime. Look at the fragment! Unfortunately, after we had a concrete proposal on the table, the government coalition has reconsidered the relevance of the specialised court and has not supported our law. If abuse in the Slovenian banking system from the past is not addressed by the perpetrators, improvement of practices in the future is also called into question. This brings potential great pressure on the ability of the bank resolution fund. Since the proposal for amendments to the Authority Act and the Bank Resolution Fund only brings about compliance with the Bank Resolution and Forced Cessation Act, we will not oppose the bill.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u76	Thank you very much. Mag. Dusan Verbič will present the position of the SMC Group. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u77	Thank you for your word, Vice President. Dear Members, Members of Parliament, Secretary of State, very nice greeting! In December 2014, the Authority Act and the Bank Resolution Fund were adopted. The law provided for Banka Slovenije to assume the tasks of the resolution authority. At the same time, it was stipulated that the Fund for Bank Resolution should also be established. This fund represents the total assets of the banks it carries out, represents and represents the Bank of Slovenia. The funds paid into the fund by the banks represent the investment of the bank. This investment shall be paid to the bank at the time of its liquidation or in the event of the liquidation of the fund itself. The law provided that the funds of the fund could be used exclusively to save banks. These funds will only be used if there is a public benefit to their use. The law provided that the Fund’s funds could be used to finance the implementation of exceptional measures governed by the law by banking and issued by the Bank of Slovenia only if it is necessary to ensure the stability of the banking system in order to ensure the availability of funds from the Fund in relation to exceptional measures in each bank. Thus, the Authority Act and the Bank Resolution Fund set out in Article 11, paragraph 3, of the Law itself provides for the use of the Fund's funds for the resolution of banks themselves. The assets of the fund may be used as deposits to the capital instruments of the bridging bank for the purposes of the payment of its initial capital or for the purpose of ensuring its capital adequacy, payments to the capital instruments of the bank which have been issued to it by exceptional measures, provided that those capital instruments are issued for the purpose of ensuring capital adequacy covered by past losses of the bank. However, it may also apply to loans, guarantees, guarantees and other collaterals, to a bridge bank or to a bank which has been granted exceptional measures and which have previously covered past losses of the bank. As one of the resolution instruments, the Bank's Institute is now regulated by the Bank's Rescue and Forced Cessation Act. Therefore, the use of the funds of the bank resolution fund to secure the capital of the bridge bank is no longer necessary. The draft law deletes the remaining provisions of the law relating only to the provision of a resolution financing mechanism under the Single Resolution Fund. That mechanism is now governed by the Bank Rescue and Forced Cessation Act. For all of this, the Group of the Parties of the Modern Centre will support the proposal for a law amending and supplementing the Authority Act and the Bank Resolution Fund. Thank you very much.

Both have a basis in the European Bank Recovery and Resolution Directive. With its transfer, we were somewhat behind schedule in Slovenia for objective reasons. At the end of 2014, only the most urgent were regulated in the law in force. A bank resolution fund has been established, a bridge bank has been designated as an instrument for bank resolution. The Bank of Slovenia was designated as the national resolution authority. The law repealed the provisions relating to the bridge bank and part of the provisions relating to the resolution financing mechanism under the single resolution fund. At the same time, it changed the purpose of using the national bank resolution fund. The most important principle of the novel is the principle of providing funds for the orderly winding-up of banks, which is realized by the possibility of using the funds of the bank resolution fund in the process of forcible winding-up of banks. This will maintain investor access to their roles, access to basic banking services by consumers. In cases where access to applications cannot be maintained, the payment of claims will take into account the priority order. The Authority and Bank Resolution Fund Act itself was established to establish a national bank resolution fund as an investment fund involving 14 banks and 3 savings banks. The Fund was set up to provide funding for bank resolution measures during the transitional period, thereby reducing taxpayers' exposure to potential future losses in the Slovenian banking sector. Since 2016, Banka Slovenije has managed this fund separately from its assets. ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u89	Dear colleagues, dear colleagues! In 2006, the Income Act introduced agricultural subsidies which were not intended for direct investment as an income base. Subsidies are intended to be paid for the implementation of activities, cross compliance, good agricultural practices and food production. ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u90	Thank you very much. I give the floor to the representative of the Government, Mr Tiln Bozic, State Secretary at the Ministry of Finance, to present my position on the proposal of the working body. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u91	Dear President, honourable Members, welcome! This proposal provides for a number of actions aimed at increasing the benefits or exemptions for debtors who report a basic agricultural and basic forestry activity for which income is generally assessed flatly on the basis of the cadastral income and the flat-rate assessment of income on bees. With regard to the increase in the income threshold of the first stage of the production of own agricultural crops, the Government considers that the applicable threshold of EUR 3 000 500 of the total income to be exempt constitutes an acceptable threshold of exemption, which can be justified in comparison with all other activities of natural persons who do not have such benefits, since the threshold represents approximately the income of the minimum wage or the minimum income twice the equivalent of not so small quantities of products or income, which does not exceed 80 % of the farm households that are liable to income on a flat-rate basis. With regard to the exemption of all payments under agricultural policy measures, i.e. agricultural subsidies under taxation, I should explain that subsidies which compensate for market income or income by international classifications of income from agricultural activities, for example FAO, OECD and so on, are considered to be income from activities. International comparisons confirm that all countries of the European Union, like Slovenia, in one way or another, as part of the flat-rate taxation of the tax base or as a separate part of the income for the purpose of taxing the subsidy income as part of the income of the agricultural activity. The Government of the Republic of Slovenia therefore considers that their exemption from taxation, both in terms of pursuing the principle of taxation by economic power and in terms of equal treatment of the debtors in a comparable income position, is not appropriate. In addition, we believe that the specificity of agricultural activity in the framework of the income is duly taken into account and that no subsidy is included in the taxation after the applicable arrangements to cover costs which are not taken into account in the calculation of the cadastral income. In this way, more than half of the subsidies which are of a nature of income but which are intended to compensate for income from environmentally friendly production, under difficult conditions or to compensate for the loss of income in the event of natural disasters are not already covered by taxation. With regard to the requirement for additional exemption of cadastral land income owned or used by young farmers during the first three years of business, it is appropriate to clarify that there are several specific support available to young farmers. The current European perspective also provides significant funds to young farmers. The granting of additional allowances in this group of debtors could constitute an unjustified advantage in relation to other natural persons who are in a similar situation at the start of their activities. In addition, let me explain that the extension of the investment relief to agricultural buildings is not in line with the principle of the principle of all types of activities, so that the investment relief is allocated primarily to investment, modern equipment and new technologies, but not to investment in land or buildings. With regard to the proposal to intervene in Article 154 of the Act, it is necessary to clarify that this provision has already been affected by the Constitutional Court’s decision, which concluded that the application of the average amounts of subsidies instead of the actual amounts of subsidies is unconstitutional and, in particular, that the provision of Article 154, which was regulated as a transitional provision, was to the regulation of the new system for the determination of cadastral income by the application of the Cadastrian Income Law in 2011, and that the law has therefore fixed its termination and application. Article 154 of the Income Act therefore no longer prejudices it. Dear Members and Members! In light of the above, the Government does not support the proposal for a law, as already presented on the Committee. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u92	Thank you very much. The following is a presentation of the views of the groups of Members. Ms Vojka Shergan will present the position of the Group of the Party of the Modern Centre.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u93	Thank you for your word. Say good-bye again! The proposal for a law amending the Income Act proposes to raise the income threshold of the small volume of the first stage of production of own agricultural crops, to which an income exemption of EUR 3 000 500 can be applied to EUR 5 000. The current ceiling is set so that comparable incomes with other espies and natural persons with activity are equally exempt and the threshold of EUR 3 000 500 of income is that ceiling which can be justified in the light of the principle of equality and the principle of taxation by economic power, as I have already mentioned, of all entities, espses and other natural persons with activity. However, it is also proposed that all payments from agricultural policy measures and all other payments received in connection with basic agricultural and basic forestry activities be fully exempt from taxation. It should be noted that payments under the measures of agricultural policy and other State aid which have the nature of the revenue compensation have been included in the income tax since 2005 when the available data have started to show that the share of the income from this Title in agricultural activities per year is increasing and that these payments represent already more than 40% of the income from agricultural activities. The decision is based on the fundamental constitutional principle that taxpayers in the same factual situation should be treated in the same way and on the principles of taxation under economic power and the rules of the Income Act, in order to tax all the incomes of the debtors in principle. The applicant also proposes that the cadastral income of agricultural and forestry land owned by a young farmer younger than 37 years of age shall not be included in the tax base of such undertakings for a period of three years from the acquisition of ownership. It should be noted that the proposed solution does not fall under Article 73 of the Income Act, which is intended to exempt land where income cannot be obtained because of different circumstances, which does not apply to land used by a young farmer. It should also be noted that income from basic agricultural and basic forestry activities is determined on land in use and not on land owned by the debtor. The last proposal of the applicant concerns the expansion of investment for investment in basic agricultural and basic forestry activities, including the purchase and construction of buildings for agricultural purposes. In principle, in terms of promoting investment, direct support is more supported than the enforcement of tax reliefs, as direct measures can be much more targeted and selective in terms of actual needs, pursuing strategic public objectives. For all of this we support the conclusion that the proposal for a law amending the Income Act is not suitable for further consideration. Thank you.

However, it should be noted that this Article no longer applies and therefore cannot be amended as this Article was in force as a transitional period until the implementation of the Cadastrian Income Act. When we adopted the Cadastrian Income Act, this 154th article was also consumed in the Cadastrian Income Act. Here I would only remind you of the discussions when we discussed the introduction of a new Cadastrian Income Act, the conclusion was, in fact, that it brought, in particular, the first phase of this Act more favourable conditions for agricultural economies than had been established before the adoption of the new Cadastrian Income Act. However, other content relating to the exclusion of subsidies which have the nature of income from basic agricultural and forestry activities would result in discriminatory treatment of the debtors and thus raises the question of the constitutional dispute of possible solutions, in particular in relation to farms which identify income from agricultural activities by bookkeeping. Those who maintain books on a regular basis would certainly be in a discriminatory position. In doing so, it is not too important to point out that, with different status within the position of a farmer resulting from the adoption of this law, it is also likely that there would be problems with entrepreneurs and others who are engaged in activities as legal persons. Thus, it can be found that, at the existing threshold for the first processing of own crops exempted from taxation – this threshold is EUR 3 000 500 – this threshold is quite comparable to, for example, the threshold for the exemption for personal supplementary work of EUR 3 000 150 per year. The current solutions are somehow consistent, harmonised and we must therefore be very careful before we interfere with the tax system, so that we do not create additional uncertainties and, above all, the discriminatory treatment of individual debtors. All these are the reasons why we voted in favour of not being able to deal with the law at the meeting of the parent working body. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u105	Thank you for your word. Nice to meet you again. The Committee on Finance and Monetary Policy is held at its 37th session. 5. 2017 was treated as a parent labour body by the proposal for a law amending the Value Added Tax Act submitted to the National Assembly for consideration by the unrelated Member Andrej Chuš. The Committee was attended by representatives of the Ministry of Finance, the Legal and Legislative Service, the Commission of the State Council for Economic Affairs, Crafts, Tourism and Finance. The Committee took note of the opinion of the Legislative and Legal Service, which examined the draft law in terms of compliance with the Constitution, the legal system and the legislative technical aspects. The service made some comments on the title of the law and the individual articles and pointed out that the status of the young farmer has been regulated. The Committee also received the opinion of the Commission of the State Council for the Economy, Crafts, Tourism and Finance and the opinion of the Government. In its opening remarks, the representative of the proposed law-maker explained that the aim of the proposed law is to introduce a higher income limit for the mandatory management of bookkeeping and the exemption of young farmers from taxation for a period of three years from the acquisition of the farm. It is also important to contribute to the resolution of the existing situation of young people launching agricultural activities and to improving the age structure of the agricultural sector. The State Secretary at the Ministry of Finance explained the reasons why the Government does not support the bill, namely that the thresholds of EUR 50 000 of taxable transport and EUR 7 000 500 of cadastral income over which both categories of small taxpayers are necessarily included in the VAT system are comparable. All income relating to basic agricultural and basic forestry activities in a farm household, as defined by the law governing income, is considered to be catastronomic income. The calculation of the threshold shall take into account all the taxable income of the farm household on the basis of basic agricultural and basic forestry activities. The raising of the threshold of cadastral income for a household farmer to EUR 15 000 would put the other small taxpayers at a disadvantage, who are exempt from the charging and payment of VAT up to the threshold of taxable transport of EUR 50 000. As a result, the threshold in question should also be raised for these debtors. The threshold of €15 000 of cadastral income would theoretically mean more than €100,000 of taxable transport. Tax policy is one of the most important indicators of the attractiveness of the business environment and can be one of the tools for stimulating the economy as a whole. Nevertheless, tax policy cannot and must not be a key indicator of the attractiveness of the business environment of a given country, but can only support other measures to ensure an attractive business environment. In the debate, some members of the Committee expressed their misunderstandings about the opposition to the law, as the higher threshold would also be a higher tax profit for the state budget and would lack concrete calculations, as was the case in the case of the consideration of the proposal for an income law. On the basis of the comments of the Legislative and Legal Service and the opinion of the Government, the Committee further proposed that the Committee, in accordance with the second paragraph of Article 128 of the Rules of Procedure of the National Assembly, discuss and decide on a proposal for a decision that the proposal for a law would not be suitable for further consideration. With 12 for and 2 against, the Committee adopted the following decision: a proposal for a law amending the Value Added Tax Act, a second treatment, is not appropriate for further consideration. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u108	I give the floor to the representative of the Government, State Secretary at the Ministry of Finance, Mr Tiln Bozic, to present my position on the proposal of the working body.
ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u109	Dear President, honourable Members, welcome back! The proposal for a law amending the Value Added Tax Act proposes a higher income limit for compulsory bookkeeping or registration for VAT purposes, resulting in 7 000 500 to 15 000 euros and exemption from VAT accounting for young farmers. There are several matters to be clarified in this regard. In accordance with the applicable value added tax law, certain taxable persons are exempt from VAT accounting on the basis of a specific scheme for small taxpayers. This is optional and is available for all debtors under the same conditions. Young farms can also be included here. The VAT Directive does not allow for exemption on the basis of the different status proposed when it comes to young farmers. For this reason, too, the possibility of a solution was used, which provides for the exemption of VAT accounting for taxable persons who do not reach a certain threshold for taxable traffic. This exception is therefore not linked to the specific status of the taxable person, but to the scope of the business and is intended for the administrative disposal of small taxpayers, irrespective of their status. It is also worth mentioning certain existing support mechanisms for young farmers, which, inter alia, provide for, for example, a sub-programme in the rural development programme, which provides support for the start-up of activities for young farmers amounting to more than €60 million in the 2014-2020 perspective. As a small taxpayer, rural households can be exempt from VAT accounting on condition that they do not keep books under the law governing income and that the cadastral income of all members of the agricultural household for the last marketing year does not exceed EUR 7 000 500. However, a special arrangement for other small taxpayers who are obliged to keep records in accordance with current economic legislation is possible if they have not exceeded or are unlikely to exceed EUR 50 000 in tax transport over the last 12 months. The marginal amounts are set in a way that compares them. This implements the principle of equal tax treatment of taxpayers. 7 000 500 Euros of cadastral income corresponds to about 50,000 Euros, resulting in 15 000 Euros corresponding to around 100,000 Euros of transport. As the Government, it is estimated that the current threshold of EUR 7 000 500 of cadastral income at this time is appropriate and comparable. Of course, we agree that the effects of the threshold of EUR 7 000 500 of cadastral income must be monitored and the threshold should be adjusted in the future, if necessary, to the changes in the amount of the taxable income of basic agricultural and basic forestry activities as a result of the transitional period under the law governing cadastral income. This assumes that this is gradually rising and is certainly an occasional reflection on whether this limit should also be adjusted. Talks will or will be under way. Taking into account the above, the Government opposes the proposal of this Act and also proposes that the proposal for a law amending the Value Added Tax Act should not be supported. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2017-05-23-SDZ7-Redna-30.ana.u111	Thank you for your word. The bill raises the threshold set out in Article 24, paragraph 2, of the Value Added Tax Act from EUR 7 000 500 of cadastral income to EUR 15 000 in order to ensure that farmers’ households no longer need to be led by bookkeeping up to the threshold of EUR 15 000. The relevant threshold for a farmer is the threshold over which those taxable persons are identified for VAT purposes and subject to the same rules as all other taxable persons, espies, parts included in the VAT system. Including the obligation to keep books. In the SMC Group, we believe that raising the threshold of cadastral income for a household farmer to EUR 15 000 would make it worse for other small taxpayers, such as espouses, parts and others, who are exempt from the charging and payment of VAT up to the threshold of taxable transport of EUR 50 000. These two thresholds are comparable. Given that the cadastral income from a real level will be slightly reduced in 2017 and that the cadastral share of the cadastral income on taxation will be gradually increased only in 2019, we agree with the Government to monitor the effects of the threshold of EUR 7 000 500, but it would be appropriate to actually adjust the threshold to the changes in the amount of the taxable income of basic agricultural and basic forestry activities only when the results of the income analysis indicate this. It is thus clear that the current threshold is appropriate because in 2015 there were 52 000 495 taxpayers in the VAT system of a total of 106 000 8 debtors whose revenues did not exceed the limit of EUR 50 000. With a threshold of EUR 100,000, about 17,000 would be able to withdraw from the VAT system, with the government assessing that about a tenth of these taxpayers, that is about a thousand 700, would actually decide to withdraw from the VAT system. The proposal for the amendment of the Value Added Tax Act, tabled by Ambassador Andrej Chus, is meaningfully linked to the amendment of the Income Act of the same proposer. This is a pursuit of the aim of reducing tax and administrative pressures on Slovenian farms. The New Value Added Tax Law raises income limits for compulsory accounting of DDD from €7 000 500 of the cadastral income of all members of the farm household to €15 000 and consequently exempts the mandatory management of accounting for a larger round of Slovenian farms. However, all persons with the status of a young farmer up to the age of 37 years shall be exempt from the compulsory payment of value added tax for a period of three years from the acquisition of the farm. We see that the provisions of this bill help young farmers on the one hand and, on the other hand, contribute significantly to competitiveness for the operation of slightly smaller Slovenian farms. If we want to provide Slovenian farmers with a competitive environment, it is certainly good to study the good practices of one of the other countries. If we look at neighbouring Austria, we find that taxation of agricultural activities is, in principle, flat-rate. The flat-rate tax base is determined on the basis of the uniform value of the farm based on the valuation of land and other production conditions, such as the size of the plant or the transport position. As a result, the unit value also includes payments under agricultural policy measures, but these are not taxed on the basis of the amounts actually received. Completely flat-rate income recognition applies to farms up to a tax value of EUR 75 000, of which the tax base is 39%. Farms worth between €75 000 and €130,000 are subject to partial flat-rate income recognition by keeping a record of revenue and recognising standardised expenditure. The management of simple accounting is foreseen between 130 000 and 150 000 Euros of tax value. Only more than 150 000 euros in tax value or, in the case of more than 400 000 euros in annual transport, double accounting is mandatory. The proposal of this law at least improves the competitive position of our farms in a certain segment compared to neighbouring Austria. Colleagues and colleagues! We have a common European agricultural policy in the European Union. Here it would make sense that Slovenia actually draws closer to tax legislation and to the bureaucratic provisions themselves to those countries which are actually beneficial to the farmer, therefore, competitive. Given that the Government and the ruling coalition obviously do not have a hearing for agricultural policy, otherwise it would have voted that this law is suitable for further consideration and would have improved the law in the next parliamentary procedure, because there is actually a need for improvement, this is in some way a blow to Slovenian agriculture. Since Slovenian agriculture is based on family farms, it is also a blow or ignorance of Slovenian families. We have a very schizophrenic situation. Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Food and the first at this Ministry speaks Buy, let's buy Slovenian! Yeah, what if it's too expensive? However, if products from other countries are cheaper and certainly as good as those from other countries. Once in this area, it will be necessary to put matters in the right place, but we certainly cannot expect this from this government suit. Also in addressing this amendment of the value added tax law, the coalition proposed at the meeting of the parent working body and adopted a decision that the proposal was not suitable for further consideration. Unfortunately, this also means a message to the entire Slovenian public that the ruling coalition has no hearing to relieve the Slovenian farmer. As we are aware of the difficult situation of many Slovenian farms in Novi Slovenija, we will vote against the conclusion that the proposal for a law amending the Value Added Tax Act is not suitable for further consideration.

[[Incomprehensible]] The decision was taken at the end of the interim report on the parliamentary investigation carried out with regard to Factor Bank, Probank and Banka Slovenije, which concludes that there is a legal ambiguity in different interpretations as regards the exercise of compensation for members of administrations, supervisory councils and other responsible individuals. In the first instance, the case-law, otherwise unlawful, lists out-of-date deadlines under the Bond Code, which are considerably shorter, while the aim of the legislator was to provide for longer limitation periods by specific law in order to prevent those responsible for the creation of the bank hole from being held liable on the basis of outdated. In order to eliminate the confusion and different interpretations, the Government proposes an authentic interpretation of this Article. Avoiding liability on the basis of obsolescence would constitute an act and a violation of the general principle of compensation law, which states that everyone is responsible for his or her conduct and the damage caused. The recovery of the damage is in the public interest, as the recovery of this damage from the budget directly affects the implementation of policies in the economic, social, demographic and other fields of operation of our country. Thank you.
